</div></div></section></section><section id="sec20"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Membrane proteins and membrane-associated proteins</h3>
<p>As the largest, the most diverse and ubiquitous superfamily of cell-surface signaling proteins that function as plasma membrane embedded receptors [<a href="#b217" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b217">217</a>] that include seven membrane-spanning α-helical segments (TM) in their core [<a href="#b218" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b218">218</a>], G protein (heterotrimeric guanine nucleotide-binding protein)-coupled receptors (GPCRs), also named seven-transmembrane-helix receptors (7TMRs), are the most potential target for anticancer drug discovery [<a href="#b219" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b219">219</a>]. GPCRs combine actions of G proteins, GPCR kinases, and arrestins [<a href="#b220" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b220">220</a>], and mediate cellular response to various external stimuli [<a href="#b221" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b221">221</a>], and regulate many biological and cellular processes, such as cell growth, differentiation, migration, apoptosis, chemotaxis and exocytosis [<a href="#b222" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b222">222</a>], serving as “<em>eyes and ears of the cell</em>” [<a href="#b218" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b218">218</a>] to assure an adequate adaptive communication between external and internal environment of cells [<a href="#b221" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b221">221</a>]. GPCRs are involved in many physiological functions, but also contribute to development and progression of many cancers, including breast cancer [<a href="#b223" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b223">223</a>], regulating cell motility, growth, proliferation and cell differentiation [<a href="#b224" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b224">224</a>], immune-cell mediated functions, angiogenesis and cancer cell survival at secondary metastatic sites [<a href="#b225" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b225">225</a>]. The GPCRs downregulation or desensitization occurs in diverse ways, that could dysregulate signaling cascades mediated by G protein subunits downstream of GPCRs [<a href="#b226" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b226">226</a>] and cross-talk pathways, including the mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK)/extracellular signal-regulated kinases (ERK) [<a href="#b227" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b227">227</a>], c-Jun N-terminal kinase (JNK) [<a href="#b228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b228">228</a>], and PI13K/AKT [<a href="#b229" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b229">229</a>] that are well known for their predominant pro-oncogenic role related to cell growth, metabolism reprogramming, enhanced proliferation and inhibited apoptosis [<a href="#b229" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b229">229</a>], highlighting the putative role of G-protein-regulated MAPK pathways in genesis and progression of different cancers, including breast cancer [<a href="#b228" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b228">228</a>]. Arrestins, as scaffold proteins in GPCRs, are able to affect the balance between activation of pro-apoptotic MAPK and the anti-apoptotic AKT signaling pathways [<a href="#b230" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b230">230</a>]. In conclusion, the overexpression of GPCRs in many types of cancer was associated with tumorigenesis, while the downregulation of GPCRs, e.g., through lentivirus-mediated silenced system, could inhibit proliferation and promotes apoptosis in tumor cells, as noted in leukemia cells [<a href="#b231" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b231">231</a>]. Also, the absence of G-protein-coupled receptor (P2Y<sub>6</sub>R) expression reduces number and volume of tumors in a mouse model of CRC, by inhibition of the X-linked inhibitor of apoptosis (XIAP) through a PI3K/AKT-dependent mechanism, inducing pro-apoptotic processes [<a href="#b232" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b232">232</a>]. As a single integral membrane glycoprotein with a large extracellular domain, dipeptidyl aminopeptidase-like protein 6 (DPP6/DPPX) belongs to the dipeptidyl peptidase-like protein (DPLP) family/dipeptidyl peptidase IV family of proteins [<a href="#b233" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b233">233</a>]. DPP6 gene was associated with cancer [<a href="#b234" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b234">234</a>], sustaining cell differentiation, proliferation and carcinogenesis [<a href="#b233" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b233">233</a>]. The overexpressed DPP6 was associated with good prognoses in breast cancer patients [<a href="#b235" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b235">235</a>], due to its ability to bind to the ECM components [<a href="#b236" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b236">236</a>]. DPP6 was significantly downregulated in in clear cell renal cell carcinoma (ccRCC) in association with nodal metastasis and tumor grade [<a href="#b237" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b237">237</a>], acute myeloid leukemia (AML) and melanoma but overexpressed in colon cancer (CC) [<a href="#b237" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b237">237</a>]. DPP6 is also studied as an auxiliary subunit of voltage-gated potassium channels of the Kv4 family [<a href="#b236" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b236">236</a>]. As a second member of the calcium-dependent activator for secretion (CAPS/CADPS) protein family, the calcium-dependent secretion activator 2 (CAPS2/CADPS2) is a calcium-binding protein ubiquitously expressed in cytoplasmic vesicle membranes or on cytoplasmic side of peripheral membrane, which acts as a calcium sensor in constitutive vesicle trafficking and secretion [<a href="#b238" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b238">238</a>], being involved in calcium-binding and regulation of protein exocytosis, related to hyperactive behavior and autism [<a href="#b239" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b239">239</a>], facilitating secretion, transport and exocytosis of large dense-core vesicles (LDCVs) [<a href="#b240" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b240">240</a>], a process also necessary for cancer cell communication [<a href="#b241" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b241">241</a>]. CAPS1 overexpression inhibits hepatoma cancer cell proliferation and migration by changing the exocytosis-associated TME <em>in vitro</em> and inhibits xenograft tumor growth <em>in vivo</em> [<a href="#b242" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b242">242</a>], while its overexpression in CRC tissues induces metastasis via PI3K/Akt/GSK3β/Snail signaling pathway-mediated EMT process and poor prognosis, promoting CRC cell migration and invasion <em>in vitro</em>, liver metastasis <em>in vivo</em>, decreasing of E-cadherin and ZO-1 as epithelial biomarkers expression and increasing the expression of mesenchymal markers, such as N-cadherin and Snail [<a href="#b243" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b243">243</a>]. Inhibiting the secretion of exosomes by CAPS1 downregulation could be helpful for treatment or patients with metastatic CRC [<a href="#b244" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b244">244</a>].</p>
<p>Cancer cells use their cytoskeleton to adapt and survive in hostile environments to successfully move and metastasize [<a href="#b245" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b245">245</a>]. As cytoskeleton components involved in cell shape maintenance, organelles distribution, mitosis and meiosis, cell motility, and intracellular transport [<a href="#b246" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b246">246</a>], microtubules (MTs) are highly dynamic, hollow-tube filamentous polymers, mostly composed of tubulin, a family of globular proteins that mainly contains α- (TUBAs) and β-tubulin (TUBBs) subunits, at least seven isotypes of β-tubulin being identified in human cells [<a href="#b247" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b247">247</a>]. TUBB3 is the most investigated tubulin isotype and it is known as biomarker of poor outcome in solid malignancies [<a href="#b247" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b247">247</a>], being cited as target for breast cancer chemotherapy [<a href="#b248" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b248">248</a>,<a href="#b249" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b249">249</a>]. <em>TUBB3</em> is absent in normal mammary epithelia, but showed a great elevated expression in most tumors, while maximal <em>TUBB6</em> expression occurred in breast and is largely decreased in most tumors [<a href="#b250" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b250">250</a>]. <em>TUBB3</em> knockdown approaches in breast cancer models revealed significant reduction in their invasive/metastatic abilities [<a href="#b251" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b251">251</a>]. Spectrin domain with coiled-coils 1 (SPECC1) was also downregulated in JTB overexpressed condition. Spectrins link the actin cytoskeleton to the cell membrane, being located at the internal side of the plasma membrane [<a href="#b29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b29">29</a>]. They are essential for structural stability and maintenance of the plasma membrane, as well as for preservation of the cell shape [<a href="#b252" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b252">252</a>] and formation of lamellipodia and filopodia [<a href="#b253" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b253">253</a>]. Spectrins contribute to cell polarity, cell adhesion and apoptosis [<a href="#b254" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b254">254</a>], cell motility and proliferation [<a href="#b255" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b255">255</a>], cell spreading and cell cycle [<a href="#b252" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b252">252</a>]. The spectrin-integrin complex is important to balance adequate forces, in order to preserve a monolayered epithelium [<a href="#b256" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b256">256</a>]. Significant spectrin downregulation was correlated to decreasing cell proliferation and migration [<a href="#b254" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b254">254</a>]. In high-grade breast cancer, spectrin could serve as a biomarker [<a href="#b255" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b255">255</a>].</p></section><section id="sec21"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">ER proteome/enzymome</h3>
<p>The oxidoreductase protein disulfide isomerase (PDI) family includes multifunctional ER enzymes that play a key role in proteostasis and ER stress [<a href="#b257" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b257">257</a>], UPR, ER-mitochondria communication and balance between pro-survival and pro-death signals [<a href="#b258" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b258">258</a>] essential for tumor proliferation and cancer cell survival [<a href="#b91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b91">91</a>]. PDI mediates formation of S-S bonds and assists correct folding of the polypeptide chains in the ER lumen, but their function could be divergent in different tissues [<a href="#b257" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b257">257</a>]. PDI enzymes catalyze the cysteine-based redox reaction and assist the quality control of proteins, functioning as molecular chaperones [<a href="#b259" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b259">259</a>]. PDIA1 family members, present in ER, mitochondria, nucleus, cytoplasm and ECM [<a href="#b258" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b258">258</a>], play a differential role in redox state regulation in the ERα-positive MCF7 cells compared to TNBC MDA-MB-271 breast cancer cell lines; PDIA1 is a pro-apoptotic factor in MCF7 breast cancer cell line and a pro-survival factor in TNBC cell line [<a href="#b258" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b258">258</a>]. The most evidences suggest that PDI is involved in proliferation, cancer cell survival, and metastasis, being upregulated in a variety of cancer types, including breast cancer, emerging as a therapeutic target for cancer therapy [<a href="#b259" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b259">259</a>]. PDI is involved in the UPR pathway [<a href="#b257" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b257">257</a>] and it is upregulated by the UPR as a cancer cell survival mechanism [<a href="#b260" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b260">260</a>], the chronic activation of UPR in tumor cells being a mechanism of tumor progression [<a href="#b261" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b261">261</a>] in malignant cell phenotypes [<a href="#b262" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b262">262</a>]. PDI knockdown-induced cytotoxicity and cell death is cell-line-dependent and involves caspase-dependent apoptosis in MCF7 cells [<a href="#b263" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b263">263</a>], accumulation of unfolded and misfolded proteins and ER stress [<a href="#b260" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b260">260</a>].</p></section><section id="sec22"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Nuclear proteome</h3>
<p>Tumor development and progression depends on components of the nuclear envelope environment associated to many signaling molecules [<a href="#b264" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b264">264</a>]. Depletion of LEM domain-containing protein 2 (LEMD2)/nuclear envelope/transmembrane protein (NET25) in cancer cells caused elevated levels of phosphorylation of ERK1 following EGF exposure [<a href="#b264" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b264">264</a>] and hyperactivation of the EGF pathway [<a href="#b265" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b265">265</a>]. ERK1 is a serine-threonine protein kinase belonging to the mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK) family, which participates in intracellular signal transduction [<a href="#b266" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b266">266</a>]. In MCF7 breast cancer cell line [<a href="#b267" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b267">267</a>] and in TNBC cells [<a href="#b268" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b268">268</a>], activation of ERK signaling pathway or high ERK protein expression levels affect the regulation of several transcription and translation factors and promotes cell growth and abnormal proliferation, EMT, angiogenesis, cell differentiation and malignant transformation, cell cycle regulation [<a href="#b266" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b266">266</a>], cell survival, and facilitates cell migration, metastasis to lymph nodes, higher recurrence rate and shorter survival [<a href="#b268" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b268">268</a>]. Contrariwise, depending on its activity in specific subcellular compartments, presence of ROS, cell and stimulus type, ERK signaling pathway mediates several antiproliferative processes, such as apoptosis, autophagy and senescence [<a href="#b269" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b269">269</a>]. High mobility group (HMG) nucleosome binding domain 1 (HMGN1) is a nucleosome binding protein that dinamically regulates the structure of chromatin fiber in response to DNA lesions, the level of histone PTMs, gene expression, and contributes to the induction of innate and adaptive immunity, acting as an alarmin that promotes antitumor responses [<a href="#b270" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b270">270</a>]. The loss of HMGNs can lead to cancer and altered immune functions [<a href="#b271" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b271">271</a>]. A reduced expression of HMGN1 was identified in metastatic breast cancer compared with low metastatic cells [<a href="#b272" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b272">272</a>].</p></section><section id="sec23"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Mitochondrial proteome</h3>
<p>ATP synthase mitochondrial F1 complex assembly factor 1 (ATPAF1) is an intra-mitochondrial-transporting protein [<a href="#b273" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b273">273</a>]. The mitochondrial ATP synthase functions are associated with cell proliferation, migration, metastasis and mitochondria dependent cell death in cancer models [<a href="#b274" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b274">274</a>]. ATPAF1 has a key role for ATP synthase assembly and mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation [<a href="#b275" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b275">275</a>]. ATPAF1 was overexpressed in prostate cancer tissue, but knockdown of this gene should significantly impair growth and proliferation under androgen-deficient condition [<a href="#b276" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b276">276</a>]. Mitochondrial NAD kinase 2 (mNADK/NADK2) catalyzes the phosphorylation of nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide (NAD<sup>+</sup>) to form <em>de novo</em> nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NADP<sup>+</sup>) that is then reduced to the antioxidant NADPH required for biosynthesis of macromolecules, protection from OS and survival or rapid growth of cancer cells [<a href="#b277" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b277">277</a>], known to preserve high levels of NADPH [<a href="#b278" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b278">278</a>]. NADK2/mNADK is responsible for the maintenance of the mitochondrial NADPH pool [<a href="#b279" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b279">279</a>], attenuates OS in mitochondria [<a href="#b278" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b278">278</a>], playing a key role in controlling the ROS level locally generated and expressed in tumor cells [<a href="#b280" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b280">280</a>]. The overproduction of ROS initially can induce uncontrolled cancer cell proliferation, DNA damage/genomic instability that supports a metastatic phenotype [<a href="#b281" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b281">281</a>], but high levels of ROS leads to cell apoptosis [<a href="#b278" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b278">278</a>]. NADH, NADPH and high levels of NADP<sup>+</sup> inhibit NADK2 [<a href="#b282" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b282">282</a>], whose silencing impairs the NADPH pool and suppress cancer cell growth [<a href="#b278" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b278">278</a>]. NADK2 deficiency reduced cell growth and proliferation in many cancer cell lines, including T47D breast cancer cell line, NADK2 activity being required for de novo proline biosynthesis and for maintenance of proline levels [<a href="#b283" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b283">283</a>]. Disruption of proline synthesis in cancer cells inhibits protein production, decreasing tumor cells viability and tumor growth [<a href="#b284" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b284">284</a>]. Inhibitors of NADK might be promising anticancer agents [<a href="#b279" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b279">279</a>].</p></section><section id="sec24"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Lisosomal proteome</h3>
<p>α-L-iduronidase (IDUA) is a lysosomal hydrolase that was significanly downregulated in breast cancer patients with visceral metastasis compared with those without visceral metastasis [<a href="#b285" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b285">285</a>]. The deficiency in IDUA results in abnormal lysosomal accumulation of glycosaminoglycans (GAG), like heparan and dermatan sulfates (DS) [<a href="#b286" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b286">286</a>], GAGs and proteoglycans being involved in all stages of tumorigenesis and cancer progression, such as cancer cell proliferation and growth, invasion and metastasis, angiogenesis, and preservation of cancer cell stemness [<a href="#b287" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b287">287</a>]. Cancer epithelia, including breast cancer with epithelial origin, emphasizes overexpressed sulfated proteogycans and DS, which could enhance the invasion of cancer cells [<a href="#b288" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b288">288</a>], the accumulation of DS in cancer epithelia remodeling the collagen around cancer cells, resulting in changes in cell shape and invasiveness through fibrillar ECM that surround them [<a href="#b288" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b288">288</a>].</p></section><section id="sec25"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Lipid metabolism-related proteome</h3>
<p>Inhibition of lipogenesis induces apoptosis and represses cancer cell proliferation [<a href="#b289" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b289">289</a>]. Phosphatidic acid/phosphatidate phosphatase (PAP) catalyzes the dephosphorylation of phosphatidic acid/phosphatidate (PA) to diacylglycerol (DAG), being involved in phospholipid synthesis and signaling [<a href="#b290" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b290">290</a>]. PAP lipins also functions as co-transcriptional regulators [<a href="#b289" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b289">289</a>]. Lipin-1 (LPIN1) is an enzyme that displays PAP activity involved in triacylglycerols (TAGs) and phospholipids (PLs) synthesis pathway and maintenance of ER homeostasis, that was overexpressed in basal-like TNBC cell lines [<a href="#b91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b91">91</a>], prostate tumor samples and cell lines [<a href="#b289" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b289">289</a>], and lung adenoma cancer cells (LUAD) [<a href="#b291" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b291">291</a>], in correlation with the poor prognosis of these patients. Lipin-1 can be localized in cytosol, ER and the nucleus [<a href="#b292" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b292">292</a>]. The knockdown pf LPIN1 increased apoptosis in TNBC cell lines [<a href="#b91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b91">91</a>], and decreased cell viability and proliferation of LUAD cells [<a href="#b291" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b291">291</a>], significantly inhibiting tumor growth <em>in vivo</em> [<a href="#b91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b91">91</a>]. Lipin-1 silencing increased autophagy and ER stress, and significantly represses prostate and breast cancer proliferation and migration [<a href="#b293" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b293">293</a>]. Lipin-1 was upregulated in BC samples compared with adjacent normal tissues and its inhibition reduces the migration of MCF7 breast cancer cells [<a href="#b292" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b292">292</a>]. Proliferating cells exhibits an aberrant FA metabolism [<a href="#b294" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b294">294</a>], the deregulated lipolysis perturbing energy homeostasis and contributing to the different diseases, including breast cancer [<a href="#b295" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b295">295</a>]. Patatin-like phospholipase domain-containing protein 2 (PNPLA2)/adipose triglyceride lipase (ATGL), an enzyme that belongs to the patalin-like phospholipase domain containing (PNPLA) family [<a href="#b296" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b296">296</a>], mainly catalyzes the first step of intracellular TAGs hydrolysis in lipid droplets (LDs) within adipocytes and non-adipocyte cells [<a href="#b295" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b295">295</a>]. ATGL upregulation in breast cancer was correlated with a high amount of peritumoral adipocytes that losses their lipid content, the free FAs released by adipocytes after lipolysis being transferred and stored in tumor cells as TAGs in LDs, inducing the aggressiveness of high-grade tumors [<a href="#b296" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b296">296</a>,<a href="#b297" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b297">297</a>]. Also, other studies sustain pro-neoplastic characteristics of ATGL action. ATGL overexpression significantly promotes colorectal cancer (CRC) cell growth and proliferation through enhancing the lipolytic pathway, while its knockdown inhibits the proliferation and promotes the apoptosis of cancer cells <em>in vitro</em> [<a href="#b298" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b298">298</a>]. <em>In vivo</em>, the ATGL expression was correlated with cancer aggressive features and is upregulated by contact with adipocytes [<a href="#b297" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b297">297</a>]. Thus, a significantly elevated activity of ATGL was detected in overweight patients with pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma (PDAC) in association with tumor stromal proliferation compared with non-obese patients [<a href="#b299" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b299">299</a>], as well as in obese patients with breast cancer, to whom ATGL was found to be significantly elevated in peritumoral and distant tumor adipose tissue compared with non-obese patients, ATGL promoting a more aggressive cancer phenotype [<a href="#b300" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b300">300</a>]. An increased expression of ATGL in breast cancer cells that were co-cultivated with mature adipocytes significantly enhanced the migration and invasion abilities of cancer cells, while abrogation of ATGL attenuated their malignancy [<a href="#b301" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b301">301</a>]. Very-long-chain (3R)-3-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydratase 2 (HACD2) regulates long-chain fatty-acyl-CoA biosynthetic process [<a href="#b302" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b302">302</a>] in the ER membrane. The HACD1-4 isozymes catalyze the third step, dehydration, in FAs four-step elongation cycle and disruption of HACD2 reduced FAs elongation toward both saturated and unsaturated FAs [<a href="#b303" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b303">303</a>]. Overexpressed JTB protein was correlated with an elevated level of hemochromatosis protein (HFE) that leads to iron accumulation in cancer cells and cancer-associated cells that stimulates breast tumor growth. Moderate levels of ROS are protumorigenic, while excessive ROS leads to cancer cell death [<a href="#b280" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b280">280</a>]. High iron levels induce lipid peroxidation and iron-dependent accumulation of ROS to lethal levels in cancer cells, also named ferroptosis. Ferroptosis is correlated with a high expression of ferroptosis-related long chain acyl CoA synthetase 4 (ACSL4) gene, also related to better overall survival, associated with significant downregulation of HACD2 protein after interfering ACSL4 transfected into MCF7 and other breast cancer cell lines [<a href="#b302" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b302">302</a>]. It could be possible that JTB overexpression acts in a comparable manner as ACLS4 overexpressed gene to increase apoptosis, as well as ferroptosis, respectively, in transfected MCF7 breast cancer cell line. It is well known that while ACSL4 plays a tumor suppressive role by suppressing tumor survival and invasiveness by promoting ferroptosis [<a href="#b304" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b304">304</a>], JTB overexpression enhances cancer cell apoptosis [<a href="#b305" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b305">305</a>], both antitumorigenic mechanisms of programmed cell death being associated with HACD2 downregulation.</p></section><section id="sec26"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Glucose metabolism-related proteome</h3>
<p>The increase in aerobic glycolysis/glycolytic flux is one of the most important hallmarks of cancer [<a href="#b306" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b306">306</a>]. Triosephosphate isomerase (TPI/TPIS) is one of the key glycolytic enzyme that is essential in carbohydrate metabolism and energy production in all living cells [<a href="#b306" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b306">306</a>], taking part in gluconeogenesis, pentose phosphate pathway (PPP) and FAs biosynthesis [<a href="#b306" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b306">306</a>]. TPI is overexpressed in many cancers, including IDC [<a href="#b307" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b307">307</a>] or HER-2/<em>neu</em>-positive breast tumors and cell lines [<a href="#b93" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b93">93</a>], and it is involved in cell growth and maintenance, increased proliferation, migration and invasion of tumor cells, being associated with shorter patients survival [<a href="#b308" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b308">308</a>]. TPI participates in cell cycle activation and is responsible for ATP production by glycolysis [<a href="#b309" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b309">309</a>]. Using a proteomic approach, autoantibodies against TPI have been detected in sera from breast cancer patients [<a href="#b310" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b310">310</a>]. Upregulated TPI expression was identified in axillary lymph node metastasis compared to node-negative breast carcinomas [<a href="#b311" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b311">311</a>]. The inhibition or slow-down of TPI activity decreased invasion and mobility in cancer cells [<a href="#b312" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b312">312</a>].</p></section><section id="sec27"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Ubiquitin-mediated degradation system</h3>
<p>Cancer may be associated with the loss of the regulator function of the ubiquitin-mediated degradation system [<a href="#b313" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b313">313</a>], that involves ubiquitination as an attachment of a small protein modifier, such as ubiquitin (Ub) and ubiquitin-like proteins (UBLs), to a protein-substrate to regulate its degradation [<a href="#b314" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b314">314</a>]. Various tumorigenic processes, such as DNA repair, cell cycle arrest, cell proliferation, apoptosis, angiogenesis, migration and invasion, metastasis and drug resistance are controlled by the ubiquitin-mediated degradation system, the conjugation of ubiquitin to a target protein for proteolysis being sequentially mediated by three classes of enzymes involved in a three-step enzymatic process: the Ub-activating enzyme E1, Ub-conjugating enzyme E2 and a Ub ligase E3 [<a href="#b314" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b314">314</a>]. Ubiquitin-activating enzyme E1-domain containing 1 (UBE1DC1/UBA5 [<a href="#b315" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b315">315</a>]) is an ubiquitin-activating enzyme [<a href="#b316" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b316">316</a>] that was widely expressed in most of human adult tissues [<a href="#b313" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b313">313</a>], and could activate different ubiquitin-like proteins, such as ubiquitin-fold modifier 1 (UFM1) and small Ub-like modifier (SUMO2), the last one being activated in the nucleus or transferred such as activated-SUMO2 to nucleus after conjugation with UBE1DC1 in cytoplasm [<a href="#b316" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b316">316</a>]. Gene encoding UBA5 was amplified in breast invasive carcinoma [<a href="#b317" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b317">317</a>]. UBA5 is involved in estrogen-induced activating signal co-integrator 1 (ASC1) UFMylation and could be used for development of new drugs against ERα-positive breast cancer as long as this process is associated with PTMs of estrogen receptor-α (ERα) as a steroid hormone-sensitive transcription factor and its co-activators, such as ASC1 target protein, which acts as a transcriptional co-activator of estrogen nuclear receptors, for promotion of transcriptional activation that leads to proliferation of different subtypes of breast tumors [<a href="#b315" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b315">315</a>].</p></section><section id="sec28"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Immunosomics</h3>
<p>Mannose/mannan-binding lectin (MBL)/mannan-binding protein (MBP) is a member of collectin (collagen-containing C-type lectins) family of proteins mainly synthetized by hepatocytes [<a href="#b318" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b318">318</a>] and occurs as circulating serum MBP (S-MBP) and intracellular MBP (I-MBP); I-MBP could function as a cargo transport lectin facilitating cytoplasmic ER-to-Golgi vesicle trafficking in glycoprotein control [<a href="#b319" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b319">319</a>]. MBL-deficient mice emphasizes defective apoptotic cell clearance [<a href="#b320" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b320">320</a>] that could lead to chronic inflammation and can contribute to tumor progression [<a href="#b321" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b321">321</a>]. Galectin-3 (Gal-3) is a multifunctional member of the non-integrin β-galactoside-binding lectin family [<a href="#b322" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b322">322</a>], ubiquitously expressed on the cell surface, cytoplasm [<a href="#b323" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b323">323</a>], nucleus, ECM [<a href="#b324" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b324">324</a>] or in circulation, the serum levels of Gal-3 being significantly higher in breast cancer patients with and without metastasis [<a href="#b325" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b325">325</a>]. Gal-3 downregulation during cancer progression, invasion and metastasis is associated with ECM remodeling, which favors the detachment of tumor cells to primary site via glycosaminoglycans regulation [<a href="#b324" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b324">324</a>]. A reduced expression of Gal-3 was correlated with a high histological grade of breast cancer due to a reduction of matrix binding and increased cancer motility [<a href="#b326" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b326">326</a>]. The absence or downregulation of GAL3 was associated with higher <em>in vivo</em> growth in murine breast tumors, metastasis, and poor prognosis [<a href="#b327" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b327">327</a>], EMT, lymphovascular invasion and cancer stemness, drug resistance and reduced overall survival in node-positive breast cancer patients [<a href="#b328" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b328">328</a>]. In early-stage breast cancer, immune profiling of the T-cell receptor beta chain (TCRβ/TCRB) repertoire highlighted that the clonal structure of the tumor is significantly different from adjacent nontumor breast tissue, with the tumor containing approximately 2.5-fold greater density of T-cells and increasing clonality compared with normal breast tissue [<a href="#b329" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b329">329</a>], while T-cell repertoire diversity in tumor was lower than in axillary lymph nodes of breast cancer patients [<a href="#b330" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b330">330</a>]. Into a TCRβ-deficient mutant cell line, the expression of the TCRα was greatly diminish, suggesting that the transcript of one gene is required for the optimal expression of another gene [<a href="#b331" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="b331">331</a>].</p></section></section><section id="sec29"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Discussion</h2>
<p>In this experiment, JTB overexpressed condition was correlated with overexpression and downregulation of a plethora of proteins involved in neoplastic behavior of MCF7 breast cancer cells. GSEA algorithm was performed to investigate the biological processes and pathways that are significantly associated with the JTB protein upregulation in MCF7 cells. The results demonstrated four significantly enriched gene sets from the following pathways that were significantly up-regulated: mitotic spindle assembly, estrogen response late, EMT and estrogen response early. Thus, the overexpressed JTB condition was significantly associated with an increased expression of ACTNs, FLNA, FLNB, EZR, MYOF, COL3A1, COL11A1, HSPA1A, HSP90A, WDR, EPPK1, FASN and FOXA1, related to cytoskeletal organization and biogenesis, mitotic spindle organization, ECM remodeling, cellular response to estrogen, proliferation, migration, metastasis, increased lipid biogenesis, estrogen-therapy resistance, and discrimination between different breast cancer subtypes. There were no significantly enriched downregulated pathways.</p>
<p>Other upregulated proteins for overexpressed JTB condition are involved in multiple cellular functions and pathways that become dysregulated, such as TME acidification (H<sup>+</sup>/K<sup>+</sup>-ATPase), the ion and other molecules transmembrane transport pathways (ABCA2, PKD1L2), glycolytic flux (TKT), iron metabolism and oxidative stress (HFE), metabolic reprogramming, nucleocytosolic mRNA transport pathways (ALY), transcriptional activation (PDCD11), chromatin remodeling (INO80), modulation of cell death pathways (USP19, Sp1), stress responsive pathways (HSPA1A, HSPA2, HSPA9, HSP90A), activation of MMPs (MYOF), cancer drug resistance (ABCA2), and pathways through which microRNAs are exported form cells (TNRC6C). Almost all these effects in breast cancer cells and cancer-associated cells promote cancer cell stemness and aggressiveness, cell cycle progression and rapid proliferation, cancer cell survival, growth, mobility, EMT and migration, invasion and metastasis, cancer cell protection against environmental hostile factors, in association with tumor progression, lymph node metastasis, distant metastasis and invasion, chemoresistance/drug and radiotherapy resistance, and poor clinical outcome of breast cancer patients. Several upregulated proteins could have a controversial role, an antioncogenic function or could improve prognosis in breast cancer patients (TPRG1L, PKD1L2, REEP2, TNRC6C, EEF1A2). Several of these upregulated proteins could be considered as promising biomarkers or putative targets for therapeutic drugs in breast cancer (MYOF, HKA, USP19, HSP90AA1).</p>
<p>The downregulated proteins for overexpressed JTB condition are involved in adaptive communication between external and internal environment of cells and maintenance between pro-apoptotic and anti-apoptotic signaling pathways (7TM/GPCRs, PDI), vesicle trafficking and secretion (CAPS2), DNA lesions repair and suppression of genes involved in tumor progression (HMGN1), proteostasis, ER stress and UPR pathway (PDI), redox state regulation (PDI, NADK2), mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation (ATPAF1), biosynthesis of macromolecules, such as the antioxidant NADPH or triacylglycerols and phospholipids (PAP/lipin-1), abnormal cellular accumulation of GAGs (IDUA), lipolytic pathway (ATGL), carbohydrate metabolism, including gluconeogenesis, PPP and FAs biosynthesis (TPI), dysregulation of ubiquitin-mediated degradation system (UBA5), cancer cell immune escape (MBL, Gal-3, and TCRβ), cell-to-cell and cell-to-ECM interactions (Gal-3), and cytoskeletal behaviour in breast cancer cells (TUBB, SPECC1). Many proteins that are downregulated in this experiment are usually overexpressed in cancer cells, including breast cancer (GPCRs/7TM, DPP6, PDI, ATPAF1, PAP, ATGL, TPI, UBA5, Gal-3, TUBB, and SPECC1). Their experimental downregulation could inhibit cancer cell growth or proliferation and promotes tumor cell apoptosis, suggesting an anti-neoplastic behavior (GPCRs/7TM, PDI, ATPAF1, NADK2, PAP, ATGL, HACD2, TPI, TUBB, SPECC1, UBA5). Downregulated DPP6, LEMD2, HMGN1, IDUA, MBL, and Gal-3 could develop a proneoplastic behavior. Several of these downregulated proteins could serve as biomarkers and novel therapeutic targets in breast cancer (GPCRs/7-TM, Gal-3, SPECC1).</p></section><section id="ack1" class="ack"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Acknowledgements</h2>

Research reported in this publication was supported by the National Cancer Institute of the National Institutes of Health under Award Number R15CA260126. The content is solely the responsibility of the authors and does not necessarily represent the official views of the National Institutes of Health.</p></section><section id="sec30"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Disclosure of conflict of interest</h2>

<section id="ref-list1_sec2"><ul class="ref-list font-sm" style="list-style-type:none">

Tumor evolutionary directed graphs and the history of chronic lymphocytic leukemia. Elife. 2014;3:e02869. doi: 10.7554/eLife.02869.</cite> [<a href="https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.02869" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">DOI</a>] [<a href="/articles/PMC4308685/" class="usa-link">PMC free article</a>] [<a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25496728/" class="usa-link">PubMed</a>] [<a href="https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?journal=Elife&amp;title=Tumor%20evolutionary%20directed%20graphs%20and%20the%20history%20of%20chronic%20lymphocytic%20leukemia&amp;volume=3&amp;publication_year=2014&amp;pages=e02869&amp;pmid=25496728&amp;doi=10.7554/eLife.02869&amp;" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Google Scholar</a>]</li>
